3. Middle East
US self-defence strikes in Syria and Iraq
In early September 2023, Syria brought to the United Nations’ attention ongoing violations of international law by the United States, highlighting the illegal presence of US forces in parts of north-eastern Syria.
Footnote
147
Syria detailed the extensive damage inflicted by US forces and associated militias, accusing them of plundering Syria’s strategic resources, including oil and minerals, amounting to losses of $115.2 billion from 2011 through mid-2023.
Footnote
148
Syria condemned these actions as a violation of its sovereignty, exacerbating the humanitarian crisis and calling for UN intervention to cease the US aggression and occupation.
Footnote
149
Further escalating tensions, on 18 September 2023, Syria reported an incident involving ‘irresponsible aggressive and provocative’ manoeuvres by two US F-16 warplanes near a Syrian civilian aircraft.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 32 | extracted HTML block 32]
150
This aggressive act was portrayed as part of a broader pattern of behaviour threatening the safety and security of international civil aviation, especially in light of similar aggressive actions by Israeli forces against civilian infrastructures in Syria.
Footnote
151
On 30 October 2023, the United States reported to the UN Security Council that it had conducted precision strikes against two facilities in eastern Syria in response to an ongoing series of armed attacks by militias affiliated with Iran against US personnel and facilities in Iraq and Syria.
Footnote
152
These military actions were described as being taken in the exercise of its inherent right of self-defence under Article 51 of the United Nations Charter.
Footnote
153
The US emphasised that these actions were necessary and proportionate, taken to deter future attacks while minimising escalation and avoiding civilian casualties.
Footnote
154
The letter noted that a military response was taken after non-military options proved inadequate to address the threat and to prevent further attacks.
Footnote
155
The United States reiterated that the US must be able to act in self-defence, ‘when, as is the case here, the government of the State where the threat is located is unwilling or unable to prevent the use of its territory by non-State militia groups responsible for such attacks’.
Footnote
156
At the UN Security Council, the United States asserted, ‘No one can argue that United States strikes were – or will be – without warning, and as United States Secretary of Defence Austin stated after the recent strikes, the United States will not hesitate to take further necessary measures to protect our people’.
Footnote
157
During the same meeting, Russia critiqued the ‘so-called right to self-defence – American-style, thousands of kilometres from United States territory’ and referred to the strikes as ‘nothing but a gross violation of Syria’s sovereignty and the norms of international law’.
Footnote
158

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 33 | extracted HTML block 33]
Syria responded further in a letter dated 9 November 2023, condemning the US military actions as a ‘crime of aggression’ and a blatant violation of Syrian sovereignty and territorial integrity.
Footnote
159
Syria dismissed the US claims as attempts to justify ongoing acts of aggression and its illegal military presence in Syrian territory, arguing that neither the UN Charter nor international law provides a legal basis for such US military actions without Syrian consent.
Footnote
160
Further, Syria declared its commitment and capability to combat terrorist organisations, notably Da’esh, highlighting its success in eradicating terrorism across most of the country, challenging the United States’ justification for its nine-year-long, unauthorised military presence in Syria.
Footnote
161
Syria concluded that it supported efforts aimed at ‘ending the attempts of the United States to misinterpret Article 51 of the Charter and abuse the application thereof in order to justify acts of aggression and threats to international peace and security’.
Footnote
162
Iran also responded, on 7 November 2023, refuting the US claims as baseless and emphasising that Iran has never directed any actions or attacks against US military forces in Syria and Iraq.
Footnote
163
Iran criticised the US’ reliance on Article 51 of the UN Charter to justify its military actions in Syria, stating this interpretation lacks legal basis.
Footnote
164
Iran underscored the United States’ violations of international law and the UN Charter within Syrian territory, noting that an occupying state cannot claim the right of self-defence to legitimise its actions on occupied land.
Footnote
165
Furthermore, Iran highlighted its presence in Syria as legal, at the Syrian Government’s request, to combat terrorism and reiterated its commitment to Syria’s sovereignty, territorial integrity, and political independence.
Footnote
166
Following up, on 14 November 2023, the US further reported to the Security Council additional measures taken in self-defence under Article 51 of the Charter, responding to continued attacks by Iranian-affiliated militias.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 34 | extracted HTML block 34]
Footnote
167
These included precision strikes on 8 and 12 November against facilities in eastern Syria used for weapons storage, among other purposes, by Iran and its affiliates.
Footnote
168
The letter underscored that these actions were again aimed at safeguarding US personnel in Iraq and Syria, and that actions in self-defence are permitted under Article 51 of the UN Charter where the ‘State where the threat is located is unwilling or unable to prevent the use of its territory by non-State militia groups responsible for such attacks’.
Footnote
169
The Syrian government responded again on 19 November 2023, reiterating its strong rejection of the US justification for its ongoing military presence and actions in Syria.
Footnote
170
The letter emphasised Syria’s call for the immediate and unconditional withdrawal of US forces, denouncing the US’ misuse of Article 51 of the Charter to justify its aggression and calling for an end to the illegal occupation and the coercive measures imposed by the US against the Syrian people.
Footnote
171
Iran also responded, on 20 November 2023, and firmly rejected the allegations laid out by the US as unfounded, reiterating that it has not participated in any acts or attacks against US military forces in Syria or elsewhere.
Footnote
172
In its communication, Iran referred to the Syrian communication dated 9 November 2023, which labels the US actions and presence in Syria as a ‘crime of aggression’, highlighting the baselessness of the US’ justification for its aggressive actions.
Footnote
173
In a letter dated 28 November 2023, addressed to the UN Security Council, the US reported further actions taken in response to armed attacks by militia groups affiliated with Iran against US personnel and facilities in Iraq and Syria.
Footnote
174
The letter detailed that these defensive measures were undertaken as an exercise of the US’ ‘inherent right of self-defence, as reflected in Article 51 of the Charter of the United Nations’.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 35 | extracted HTML block 35]
175
Following the continuation of hostilities since a prior notification on 14 November, the US executed targeted strikes on the evening of 21 November against Iranian-affiliated facilities in Iraq, describing the response as ‘necessary and proportionate’ to limit escalation risks.
Footnote
176
The letter emphasised the US’ readiness to conduct further actions necessary to protect against future threats or attacks on its nationals, personnel, and facilities under its inherent right of self-defence.
Footnote
177
In response, Syria accused the United States of attempting to deflect from its support of what Syria termed ‘Israeli terrorism’, denouncing it as a manifestation of ‘political hypocrisy and double standards’.
Footnote
178
Highlighting the history of US military interventions in countries such as Vietnam, Afghanistan, Iraq and Libya, Syria criticised the US for citing Article 51 of the UN Charter to legitimise its ‘ongoing crimes’ within Syrian borders.
Footnote
179
Syria argued that the Charter and international law do not sanction the presence of US forces in Syria without Syria’s consent,
Footnote
180
Iraq also responded, describing the US drone strikes as a dangerous escalation and a clear violation of its sovereignty.
Footnote
181
The attack, resulting in several deaths and injuries, was deemed unacceptable by Iraq, highlighting the detrimental impact on Iraq’s security, regional stability, and Iraq-US bilateral relations.
Footnote
182
Iraq refuted the rationale that the strikes were aimed at protecting American forces, stating there was no legitimate legal basis for this action.
Footnote
183

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 36 | extracted HTML block 36]
It emphasised that the presence of US forces was intended solely for support, advising, training, and rehabilitating the Iraqi armed forces, under the Iraqi Government’s invitation.
Footnote
184
Consequently, Iraq asserted that any military actions within its territory must be coordinated and consulted with the Iraqi Government.
Footnote
185
Iran also issued a response, on 4 December 2023, firmly rejecting ‘the continued unsubstantiated allegations’ made by the US.
Footnote
186
Iran, once again, reiterated its stance that it has not engaged in any actions or attacks against US military forces, criticising the US allegations as an attempt by the occupying state to legitimise its ongoing violations of international law and the UN Charter in Syria.
Footnote
187
Following the escalation, a further communication from the United States to the UN Security Council on 29 December 2023 detailed additional measures taken in self-defence, pursuant to Article 51 of the United Nations Charter.
Footnote
188
This action was in reaction to alleged continuous armed attacks by militia groups linked to Iran continuing to target US personnel and facilities in Iraq and Syria.
Footnote
189
The letter highlighted recent attacks, specifically mentioning an assault by Kata’ib Hezbollah and affiliated factions on US and coalition forces at the Erbil airbase on 25 December 2023, which resulted in injuries to US personnel.
Footnote
190
The US described these measures as necessary and proportionate to prevent future attacks, reiterating its commitment to take further actions in the exercise of its inherent right of self-defence to respond to future attacks or threats against its nationals and personnel.
Footnote
191
US – Iran: Allegations of Security Council resolution violations and maritime security concerns

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 37 | extracted HTML block 37]
On 4 August 2023, the United States reported to the UN Security Council the alleged ongoing transfer of hundreds of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) from Iran to Russia, in violation of United Nations Security Council resolution 2231 (2015).
Footnote
192
The US expressed concern over Russia’s use of these Iranian-made drones to intensify bombardments in Ukraine, specifically targeting grain infrastructure.
Footnote
193
Following this, on 7 August 2023, in a separate letter, the US underscored concerns over Iran’s actions threatening navigational rights and freedoms, and international maritime security, particularly through attacks, seizures, and harassment of commercial vessels near the Strait of Hormuz.
Footnote
194
The US alleged that Iran’s hostile engagements constituted a clear threat to international peace and security, and called for such actions to be condemned by the Security Council.
Footnote
195
In response, on 17 August 2023, Iran addressed two letters to the UN Secretary-General refuting the US allegations. In the first letter, Iran dismissed claims of endangering maritime security, claiming that the US’ letter ‘in yet another cynical attempt, has levelled false accusations against my country by spreading disinformation and misleading narratives’.
Footnote
196
Iran stated the incident involving the vessel Richmond Voyager was a collision with an Iranian ship, causing substantial damage and injuries, contrary to US claims of aggressive manoeuvres by Iran.
Footnote
197
In the second letter on the same day, Iran contested the US’ accusations regarding the UAV transfers and involvement in Ukraine, rejecting the allegations as baseless and politically motivated.
Footnote
198
Iran criticised the US and certain Western countries for their failure to adhere to resolution 2231 (2015) and for distorting facts to advance their political agendas.
Footnote
199
On 11 September 2023, the United States escalated its concerns with the UN Security Council regarding Russia’s utilisation of Iranian drones against Ukrainian civilian infrastructure, asserting this act violates Security Council resolution 2231 (2015).

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 38 | extracted HTML block 38]
Footnote
200
The US shared intelligence analysis comparing debris from Iranian-made UAVs used in both Iraq and Ukraine to substantiate claims of Iran’s unauthorised UAV supply to Russia.
Footnote
201
In response, on 15 September 2023, Iran dismissed the US’ allegations as baseless, asserting the ‘so-called evidence’ was ‘utterly fabricated and devoid of any legal validity’.
Footnote
202
Iran accused the US of misleading the international community and manipulating the UN Secretariat to serve its political interests, while itself violating resolution 2231 (2015).
Footnote
203
Turkey – Syria: Disputes over water resources and self-defence claims
In July 2023, Syria wrote to the UN Security Council concerning alleged withholding of drinking water by the United States and Turkey of over 1 million people in Syria.
Footnote
204
Syria urgently called upon the UN to intervene, demanding the full restoration of water pumping from the Uluk station and insisting on international supervision to ensure a sustainable water supply for Hasakah and its surroundings.
Footnote
205
Furthermore, Syria emphasised that the withdrawal of US and Turkish forces was imperative to end the suffering and restore stability and humanitarian conditions in the region.
Footnote
206
Turkey, in response to Syria’s allegations, firmly rejected the claims, attributing the water supply disruption at the Uluk pumping station to electrical outages controlled by the PKK/YPG, not to any deliberate actions by Turkey or Syrian opposition.
Footnote
207
Turkey defended its counterterrorism actions as legitimate self-defence under Article 51 of the UN Charter, criticising the focus on its efforts rather than addressing the broader issues threatening Syria’s territorial integrity.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 39 | extracted HTML block 39]
Footnote
208
Turkey – Iraq: Turkey’s self-defence claims
In September 2023, Iraq reported to the United Nations a substantial number of Turkish violations of its territory and airspace, totalling some 2,000 incidents from May to July 2023.
Footnote
209
Turkey responded in late September, explaining that, ‘in the absence of Iraq’s ability to deal with the presence of terrorist organisations in its own territory, Türkiye is obliged to take appropriate measures against terrorist threats to its security emanating from Iraq’.
Footnote
210
Turkey defended its military activities as exercises of its inherent right of self-defence under Article 51 of the UN Charter, dismissing criticisms of its actions as unjustified.
Footnote
211
In a follow-up communication in December 2023, Iraq catalogued an additional 1,453 violations by Turkey from August to October 2023, reaffirming its stance that these acts violated Iraqi sovereignty and international law.
Footnote
212
Turkey, again, issued a refutation on 4 December, reiterating its justification for measures taken against perceived terrorist threats from Iraqi territory in the absence of Iraq’s ability to deal with such threats.
Footnote
213
Turkey maintained that its actions were in accordance with its right to self-defence as per international law and Security Council resolutions, asserting that criticism of its defensive measures was unacceptable.
Footnote
214
Lebanon – Israel: Exchange of allegations of violations
Israel and Lebanon continued in their regular exchange, via the UN, of allegations of violations of Security Council Resolution 1701 (2006) and the ‘Blue Line’.
Footnote
215

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 40 | extracted HTML block 40]
During this reporting period, Israel wrote to the Security Council alleging Lebanese violations of the Blue Line between Israel and Lebanon for April to June 2023.
Footnote
216
Lebanon, also during this reporting period, transmitted statistical summaries of alleged violations of Lebanese airspace, territorial waters and territory committed by Israel in the period February to September 2023.
Footnote
217
Lebanon also reported the alleged use by Israel of white phosphorus during military operations inside the borders of Lebanon.
Footnote
218
In July 2023, Israel reported to the UN Security Council a series of security breaches along its northern border, including an anti-tank missile fired from southern Lebanon and rocket fire in April, alongside the establishment of Hezbollah military compounds under the guise of a pseudo-environmental NGO.
Footnote
219
Israel reaffirmed that it ‘will take all necessary steps to protect its citizens’.
Footnote
220
In October 2023, amid a broader conflict involving attacks by Hamas on Israeli civilians, Israel reported further aggression from Hezbollah, including the firing of mortar shells from Lebanon into Israel.
Footnote
221
Israel held the Lebanese Government responsible for attacks emanating from its territory, emphasising Israel’s right to defend itself.
Footnote
222
Following these incidents, Israel reported an infiltration by terrorists from Lebanon and subsequent anti-tank and mortar attacks claimed by Hezbollah, viewing these as severe threats to its sovereignty and regional stability.
Footnote
223
Israel reiterated its expectation for Lebanon to prevent attacks from its territory and for UNIFIL to effectively fulfil its mandate to ensure peace.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 41 | extracted HTML block 41]
Footnote
224
Lebanon responded to these allegations in a letter dated 13 October 2023, detailing Israeli attacks on Lebanese territory, including bombings and artillery fire that targeted civilians and infrastructure.
Footnote
225
Lebanon maintained its commitment to resolution 1701 (2006) and emphasised that Israel’s attacks were disproportionate and could not be justified as self-defence.
Footnote
226
In a series of further correspondences to the UN, Lebanon outlined grave incidents attributed to Israeli actions, including a drone attack on 5 November 2023, which killed three children, their mother, and grandmother.
Footnote
227
Lebanon asserted that Israel’s actions constituted ‘clear and documented war crimes that flagrantly violate international humanitarian law, as the targeting of civilians under the pretext of self-defence is totally unacceptable’.
Footnote

For instance, on 21 November 2023, Lebanon reported the killing of two journalists by an Israeli missile, emphasising that the attack’s execution occurred deep within Lebanese territory, violating Lebanon’s sovereignty.

Israel – Iran: Tensions escalate
In August 2023, Israel reported to the UN Security Council a series of shooting attacks perpetrated by Palestinian terrorists, leading to the deaths of four Israelis and wounding another.
Footnote
230
These incidents were highlighted as evidence of a broader wave of terror, attributed to incitement by the Palestinian Authority and support from Iran to groups like Hamas and Palestinian Islamic Jihad.
Footnote

Israel declared that it would ‘take any means necessary in order to defend its citizens’.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 42 | extracted HTML block 42]
Footnote
232
In response, Iran strongly condemned and categorically rejected accusations made by Israel, accusing Israel of attempting to ‘divert international attention away from the severe atrocities being perpetrated against the Palestinian people’ by making baseless accusations against Iran.
Footnote
233
In a letter dated 14 September 2024, Iran reported to the Security Council statements from Mossad Director David Barnea who claimed that Mossad had thwarted Iranian attacks abroad and threatened direct actions against Iranian officials, even within Tehran, in retaliation.
Footnote

Footnote

Iran affirmed that ‘it will not hesitate to exercise its inherent and legitimate rights to defend its security and national interests’ against any acts originating from Israel.
Footnote

Footnote

Iran stressed that such threats violate international law and the UN Charter’s Article 2(4), highlighting the serious threat this poses to international peace and security.
Footnote
238
Iran called on the Security Council to denounce Israel’s dangerous rhetoric and reaffirmed its right to defend its sovereignty and people from any Israeli-originated threats.
Footnote
239
In a letter dated 21 November 2023, Israel accused Iran of continuing activities in violation of Security Council resolution 2231 (2015).
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 43 | extracted HTML block 43]

Countering these claims, Iran’s response on 28 November 2023 strongly refuted the allegations, asserting that its space and missile programs are conducted within the bounds of international law and do not contravene Security Council resolution 2231 (2015).

Footnote

During this reporting period, Syria repeatedly wrote to the Security Council concerning numerous alleged attacks by Israel against Syria, calling upon the UN to put an end to the Israeli occupation of Syrian territory, and to compel Israel to comply with international law.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 53 | extracted HTML block 53]
In that regard, we recall in particular paragraph 139 of the advisory opinion of the International Court of Justice of 9 July 2004 on the legal consequences of the construction of a wall in the occupied Palestinian territory, which states that Israel does not have the right to self-defence in the occupied Palestinian territory (see A/ES-10/273). What applies in this case, however, are the provisions of international humanitarian law pertaining to a military response to armed attacks in occupied territories.
Footnote
321

Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 58 | extracted HTML block 58]

Legal consequences of the construction of a wall in the occupied Palestinian territory
(see A/ES-10/273), is inapplicable in the case of an occupying Power, which is what Israel is with regard to the Palestinian territory.

Footnote

On 26 October 2023, Russia appealed to the United Nations and global parliaments to halt the escalating violence in the Palestinian-Israeli conflict, emphasising the urgent need to stop the bloodshed and ‘prevent an unprecedented humanitarian catastrophe’, particularly after a missile attack on a Gaza hospital that killed hundreds of civilians.
Footnote
381
On the same day, Turkey vehemently dismissed allegations of antisemitism by some Israeli officials as unfounded slander, decrying their attempts to deflect from the massacre of Palestinian civilians in Gaza through misleading distortions.
Footnote
382
It urgently called on Israeli authorities to end the barbarism which Turkey claimed ‘aims to completely destroy the people of Gaza’.
Footnote
383
Turkey criticised, what it referred to as, the siege and inhumane attacks on Gaza, stating that Israel aimed to deprive the Palestinian people of their most basic rights which clearly violated international law.
Footnote
384
On 30 October 2023, Israel declared its refusal to continue living with what it described as ‘brutal Hamas Nazis’ on its border.
Footnote
385
Israel emphasised that its operations in Gaza, which it likened to actions against ‘savage ISIS Hamas death squads’, were essential acts of self-defence to secure its future, not merely responses to the events of 7 October.

Many Council members talk about one narrative, about one State to defending itself. When our children are being killed, do we have the right to defend them? Do we have the right to defend our families? Do we have the right to defend our schools? Do we have the right to defend our mosques? Do we have the right to defend our churches? Do we have the right to defend our homes, our soil and our nation? Do we have rights or are rights only the monopoly of one side?

Footnote

We underscore that, while Article 51 preserves the inherent right to self-defence, this mechanism cannot be allowed to be used as a justification for the indiscriminate killing of civilians or to shield violations of international law. To remain within the parameters of what is legally acceptable and appropriate, self-defense must be both necessary and proportionate.
(…)
Over 9,000 people have been killed in the Gaza Strip since October 7th, with children making up more than 3000 of the aggregate, and over 20,000 injured. Behind each of these numbers is a face, no less worthy of a right to life than you or me. These statistics unambiguously illustrate that the justification of self-defense has gone beyond what is proportionate and necessary, and therefore exceeded the realm of what is legally acceptable. So, I ask you – how many more human beings must die before the Security Council considers the act of exercising the veto more egregious than its moral and legal obligation of “saving succeeding generations from the scourge of war”?

He called for the release of 200 hostages, taking more than 2 million hostages in the process. He demanded condemnation of the killing of 1,000 Israeli civilians and killed 10,000 Palestinian civilians in the process. He proclaimed his right to self-defence while occupying, colonizing and denying every right to the Palestinian people, including our right to self-determination, our right to protection and our right to defend ourselves.

Invocation of Article 99 of the UN Charter invocation and the unfolding humanitarian crisis
On 20 October 2023, the Group of Friends of the Responsibility to Protect (R2P), consisting of 55 Member States and the European Union, delivered a statement underscoring the international community’s obligation to utilise diplomatic and peaceful means under the UN Charter and the 2005 World Summit Outcome to prevent mass atrocities globally.
Footnote
434
They advocated for the Secretary-General’s proactive engagement in prevention and peaceful dispute resolution, highlighting the importance of Article 99 in alerting the Security Council’s attention to ‘any risk of the commission of genocide, war crimes, ethnic cleansing or crimes against humanity that in his opinion might threaten the maintenance of international peace and security’.
Footnote
435
The statement emphasised the broader responsibility to protect populations, and did not make any reference to a particular incident.
The UN Secretary-General, António Guterres, invoked Article 99 of the Charter of the United Nations in a letter dated 6 December 2023.
Footnote
436

Footnote

Footnote

Footnote

4. Asia-Pacific
Azerbaijan – Armenia: ‘Counter-terror measures’ and integration of Nagorno-Karabakh with Azerbaijan
The relations between Azerbaijan and Armenia have remained tense since the outbreak of the armed conflict in 2020. Previous Digests covered some of these hostilities.
Footnote
467
In the current reporting period, the clashes continued. Azerbaijan accused ‘the Armenian armed forces of illegally remaining in the territory of Azerbaijan’
Footnote
468
and intensifying ‘the military engineering works and other military build-up’.
Footnote
469
Then, according to Azerbaijan, on 16 August ‘a reconnaissance group of Armenian armed forces attempted to infiltrate the territory of Azerbaijan and carry out diversion operations in the Kalbajar District in Nagorno-Karabakh’.
Footnote
470
Two civilians and four police officers died in incidents involving landmines, allegedly placed by Armenian armed forces.
Footnote
471
In response to these events, on 19 September Azerbaijan announced that
the armed forces of Azerbaijan launched local counter-terror measures to disarm those illegal armed formations and neutralize the imminent threat posed to the safety and security of Azerbaijani civilian and military personnel. The counter-terror measures were limited and proportionate to the threat posed to Azerbaijan’s sovereignty and territorial integrity, as well as the safety and well-being of our citizens. The counter-terror measures were aimed exclusively at neutralizing legitimate military targets in the sovereign territory of Azerbaijan. As such, they were fully aligned with the sovereign right of Azerbaijan to self-defence enshrined in the Charter of the United Nations. Azerbaijan acted in full compliance with the norms of humanitarian law.
Footnote
472

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 72 | extracted HTML block 72]
Armenia however named Azerbaijan’s action a ‘blatant violation of international law and the trilateral statement of 9 November 2020’,
Footnote
473
as well as claimed that
the whole territory of Nagorno-Karabakh, Stepanakert and other cities and settlements came under intense and indiscriminate shelling, with the use of missiles, heavy artillery and combat unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) and aircraft, including prohibited cluster munitions. The intensity and cruelty of the offensive makes it clear that the intention is to finalize the ethnic cleansing of the Armenian population of Nagorno-Karabakh.
Footnote
474
On 20 September authorities of Nagorno-Karabakh agreed to a Russian proposal for a ceasefire which entailed also the complete disarmament of their military force; at the same time, Azerbaijan regained control over the territory.
Footnote
475
A few days later the President of the Nagorno-Karabakh Republic, Samvel Shahramanyan, signed a decree to dissolve all state institutions by 1 January 2024, ahead of full integration with Azerbaijan.
Footnote
476
North Korea: Self-defence claims amid missiles and spy satellite launches
On 12 July 2023, North Korea launched a test of Hwasong-18, a solid-fueled intercontinental ballistic missile.
Footnote
477
The missile flew about 1,000 kilometres, before falling into the sea inside the Russian Federation’s exclusive economic zone. The flight was reported to be the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea’s longest-duration ballistic missile flight.
Footnote
478
The launch was condemned by several States, including the G7 States,
Footnote
479
as well as by the UN Secretary-General.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 73 | extracted HTML block 73]
480
During the debate in the UN Security Council North Korea claimed that ‘test-fire of a new type of ICBM – the Hwasong-18 – is a warranted exercise of the right to self-defence to deter dangerous military actions by hostile forces and safeguard the security of our State and peace in the region without fail’.
Footnote
481
Thus, the statement suggested that North Korea viewed the launch of the missile as the realisation of the right to preventive self-defence. Such a finding is also confirmed by the fact that North Korea did not claim that it was under an armed attack but rather that it was entitled to the right to self-defence because of ‘the hostile policy and military threats of the United States, under the single pretext of violations of Security Council resolutions — which were forcibly adopted by the United States and its followers’.
Footnote
482
North Korea also warned that ‘the United States’ attempted military attacks and aggressive actions against the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea’ instigate ‘the outbreak of a nuclear war on the Korean peninsula’.
Footnote
483
Moreover, North Korea opposed convening the UN Security Council briefing to discuss the exercise of its inherent right to self-defence as such discussion disregards the sovereignty of North Korea:
A Security Council open briefing on the legitimate exercise of the right to self-defence of a sovereign State is a contradictory action that denies the fundamental principles of sovereign equality and non-interference in the internal affairs of States, enshrined in the Charter and norms of international relations. We categorically reject and condemn the convening of this Security Council briefing by the United States and its followers, which encroaches upon the legitimate right of the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea to self-defence, since it constitutes an illegal and reckless act of violating the spirit of the Charter and international law. (…) It is an act of flagrant disregard for the sovereignty of the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea to take issue with our legitimate exercise of the right to self-defence alone, without any discussion of the validity of the overstepping military moves by the United States and South Korea.
Footnote
484
Further, on 24 August North Korea launched a Chollima-1 rocket carrying its first military reconnaissance satellite but failed to put it into orbit.
Footnote
485
Like previously, North Korea claimed that ‘launch of the reconnaissance satellite is an exercise of the legitimate right to self-defence to deter the ever-increasing hostile military acts of the United States and its followers. At the same time, such a launch is aimed at building our capabilities for self-defence’.
Footnote
486
The launch was condemned by a number of states, including Japan who rejected the self-defence argument by stating that ‘This is not about exercising the right to self-defence. This is about violations of Security Council resolutions’.
Footnote
487
On 21 November North Korea reiterated an attempt to launch a reconnaissance satellite and this time successfully placed it in orbit.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 74 | extracted HTML block 74]
488
The arguments invoked both by North Korea and by the states who criticised the launch were similar to those in August.
Footnote
489
The US reacted to the launch by saying that
The Democratic People’s Republic of Korea claims that it is acting in self-defence, but as the Council knows, that argument really does not stand up here, as the military exercises of the United States and the Republic of Korea are routine and defensive in nature. (…) Our exercises are going to continue, and they are to prepare to defend ourselves and our allies from this increasing threat from the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea.
Footnote
490
Then, on 18 December 2023 North Korea again launched Hwasong-18-type intercontinental ballistic missile.
Footnote
491
It also repeated its argument that it ‘exercised the undeniable just and legitimate rights of a sovereign State to self-defence’,
Footnote
492
and, like previously it was harshly criticised in the UN Security Council.
Footnote
493
During all the discussions in the UN Security Council concerning North Korea Russia adopted a position very favourable for the former state as it highlighted that the situation in the region escalated due to the ‘United States, the Republic of Korea and Japan, which continue to increase the scale of regional exercises and military interaction’.
Footnote
494
It also referred to the self-defence argument by stating that:
We are seeing both sides justify their mutually hostile moves in the current situation by invoking their sovereign right to self-defence. We understand the motives behind such rhetoric on the part of both Pyongyang and Seoul. However, what cannot be explained is the inclusion of the United States among the so-called victims of North Korean provocations even though its borders are thousands of kilometres away from the Korean peninsula.

Pakistan – Afghanistan: Border raid

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 75 | extracted HTML block 75]
On 6 September 2023, Pakistani troops repelled a cross-border raid from Afghanistan conducted by hundreds of Pakistan Taliban militants belonging to Tehreek-e-Taliban Pakistan (TTP). The militants, equipped with light and heavy weapons, attacked two outposts. In a statement TTP claimed that that they managed to seize them. As a result of clashes four Pakistani soldiers and twelve militants were killed.
Footnote
496
Pakistan regularly accuses Afghanistan of harbouring militants, which the latter State consistently denies.
Footnote

Taiwan: Visit of US lawmakers and scenarios of invasion by China
On 1 September 2023, Rob Wittman, vice chairman of the US House of Representatives Armed Services Committee, accompanied by four other lawmakers from the Republican Party, met in Taipei with Taiwanese President Tsai Ing-we. US lawmakers reassured Taiwan about its support and praised Taiwan’s efforts to defend itself against a potential attack from China. Wittman also said that ‘President Tsai, know that any, any hostile, unprovoked attack on Taiwan will result in a resolute reaction from the United States’.
Footnote
498
Among other developments concerning Taiwan, in October 2023 US Department of Defense published a report entitled ‘Military and Security Developments Involving the People’s Republic of China’. The report notes that ‘[a]lthough Beijing reaffirms that “peaceful reunification” is its preferred course of action, the PRC continues to signal its willingness to use military force against Taiwan’.
Footnote
499
The report claims that the People’s Liberation Army (PLA) could offer the Chinese President the following military options against Taiwan: air and maritime blockade; limited force or coercive options; air and missile campaign; amphibious invasion of Taiwan; and seizure of small islands occupied by Taiwan in the South China Sea.
Footnote
500
According to the Pentagon, ‘[t]he PLA practiced elements of each of these military options during its August 2022 large-scale military exercise aimed at pressuring Taiwan, and again in April 2023 in response to Taiwan president Tsai Ingwen’s transit of the United States’.
Footnote
501
People’s Republic of China: Development of nuclear weapons
The Pentagon report mentioned above also claims that in May 2023 China possessed more than 500 operational nuclear warheads, exceeding ‘previous projections’.
Footnote
502
The Department of Defense estimated that ‘[o]ver the next decade, the PRC will continue to rapidly modernize, diversify, and expand its nuclear forces. Compared to the PLA’s nuclear modernization efforts a decade ago, current efforts dwarf previous attempts in both scale and complexity’.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 76 | extracted HTML block 76]
503
In reaction to the Pentagon’s report, China’s foreign ministry spokeswoman Mao Ning said that ‘China firmly pursues a nuclear strategy of self-defence’. She also added that ‘No country will be threatened by China’s nuclear weapons as long as it does not use or threaten to use nuclear weapons against China’.
Footnote
504
Australia – Tuvalu: Australia-Tuvalu Falepili Union Treaty
On 9 November 2023, Australia and Tuvalu signed the Australia-Tuvalu Falepili Union Treaty.
Footnote
505
The aim of the treaty is to ‘establish a Falepili Union’, ‘provide the citizens of Tuvalu with a special human mobility pathway to access Australia’, as well as ‘protect and promote each Party’s and the Parties’ collective security and sovereignty’ (Art. 1). When it comes to the latter issue, under Article 4 of the Treaty:
1.
Australia shall, in accordance with its international law obligations, international commitments, domestic processes and capacity, and following a request from Tuvalu, provide assistance to Tuvaluin response to:
a.
a major natural disaster;
b.
a public health emergency of international concern;
c.
military aggression against Tuvalu.
2.
The Parties shall enter into an instrument to set out the conditions and timeframes applicable to Australian personnel operating in Tuvalu’s territory.
3.
In addition to the Parties’ rights and freedoms under international law, provided that advance notice is given by Australia, Tuvalu shall provide Australia rights to access, presence within, and overflight of Tuvalu’s territory, if the activities are necessary for the provision of assistance requested by Tuvalu under this agreement.
4.
Tuvalu shall mutually agree with Australia any partnership, arrangement or engagement with any other State or entity on security and defence-related matters. Such matters include but are not limited to defence, policing, border protection, cyber security and critical infrastructure, including ports, telecommunications and energy infrastructure.
Footnote
506

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 77 | extracted HTML block 77]
5. Americas
Costa Rica: Position on the application of international law in cyberspace
On 21 July 2023, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Costa Rica submitted a paper to the UN Open-ended Working Group on security of and in the use of information and communications technologies, outlining its position on the applicability of international law in cyberspace.
Footnote
507
On the use of force, the paper stated that
35. (…) In Costa Rica’s view, a State uses force against another State if it causes damage to persons or property in the territory of another State. This is true whether or not the military or other armed forces are involved, and regardless of the level of intensity of any hostilities between the States involved.
36. As noted earlier, the prohibition on the use of force applies irrespective of the type of weapon employed by a State. Thus, a cyber operation may amount to a prohibited use of force if it can cause harm or destruction analogous to a conventional weapon. In Costa Rica’s view, this assessment is to be carried out on the basis of a comparison between the effects of a cyber operation and those of an operation carried out by conventional weapons that would constitute a prohibited use of force. Although this assessment can only be carried out on a case-by-case basis, examples of cyber operations likely amounting to a prohibited use of force include those causing physical harm to individuals or significant destruction of property, as well as those permanently disabling operating systems controlling critical infrastructure, such as an electrical grid or a water and sanitation station.
Footnote
508
Emphasising that ‘[a] prohibited threat or use of force must be distinguished from an armed attack’, the paper also stated that
37. (…) In Costa Rica’s view, to give rise to the right of self-defense, an armed attack must be attributable to a State, in cyberspace as in any other context. As noted by the ICJ, armed attacks are the ‘most grave’ forms of use of force. For Costa Rica, this assessment is to be carried out on the basis of a comparison between the scale and effects of a cyber operation and those of an operation by conventional weapons that would constitute an armed attack. Examples of cyber operations potentially constituting armed attacks are those causing significant loss of life and destruction of critical infrastructure.
Footnote
509
Guyana – Venezuela: Tensions over the Essequibo region
The long-standing territorial dispute between Guyana and Venezuela over the Essequibo region which has been under the control of Guyana escalated into a new crisis in September 2023. In a statement on 19 September 2023, the Foreign Ministry of Venezuela deplored Guyana’s decision to grant drilling licenses for oil exploration in its offshore waters, saying Venezuela has legitimate claims over the maritime zone in question and it ‘will apply all the necessary measures to avoid the illegitimate exploitation of natural resources that belong to our nation’.
Footnote
510
In response, in a statement on the same day, the Government of Guyana claimed that the waters in question form part of its Exclusive Economic Zone and described Venezuela’s statement that it will ‘apply all the necessary measures’ as ‘a threat not only to Guyana but to regional and international peace and security, as well as to all of Guyana’s current and potential investment partners’.
Footnote
511
It also recalled that Venezuela is obliged under the UN Charter and the Charter of the Organization of American States ‘to employ only peaceful means to seek a resolution’ and that ‘[b]oth Charters prohibit the threat or use of force against another State’.
Footnote
512
Likewise, in a statement on 22 September 2023, the Caribbean Community (CARICOM) considered ‘the stated intention of Venezuela to “apply all the necessary measures” to prevent the operations licensed by Guyana in its waters, as a threat of the use of force contrary to international law’.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 78 | extracted HTML block 78]
Footnote
513
Meanwhile, in a statement on 20 September 2023, the Foreign Ministry of Venezuela rejected the US support for Guyana’s claims and the intentions of the US to create a military base in Guyana so that it can launch an ‘operation of aggression’ against Venezuela.
Footnote
514
On 20 October 2023, Venezuela also released its plans to put a list of five questions before the Venezuelan people in a ‘Consultative Referendum’ on 3 December 2023.
Footnote
515
One of the questions was ‘Do you agree with the creation of the Guayana Esequiba State and … consequently incorporating that State into the map of Venezuelan territory?’
Footnote
516
Upon this development, on 30 October 2023, Guyana filed a request for the indication of provisional measures by the International Court of Justice in the case concerning
Arbitral Award of 3 October 1899 (Guyana v. Venezuela)
, which was instituted by Guyana in 2018 over its border dispute with Venezuela.
Footnote
517
In its Request, Guyana described the purpose of the Referendum as ‘to resort (…) to unilateral measures to “resolve” the controversy with Guyana by formally annexing and integrating into Venezuela all of the territory at issue in these proceedings’ and asked the Court to order Venezuela not to ‘take any actions that are intended to prepare or allow the exercise of sovereignty or
de facto
control over any territory that was awarded to British Guiana in the 1899 Arbitral Award’.
Footnote
518
Guyana’s Request also cited a statement by CARICOM which stated that
the language of two questions approved to be posed in the Referendum seeks an affirmation and implementation of Venezuela’s stance on the issue “by all means, according to/with the Law.” It is open to reasonable persons to conclude that “by all means”, includes means of force or war.
CARICOM earnestly hopes that Venezuela is not raising the prospect of using force or military means to get its own way in this controversy over territory. After all, it has been the long-standing position of Latin American and Caribbean countries, including Venezuela, that our Region must remain a Zone of Peace.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 79 | extracted HTML block 79]
519
Likewise, earlier, in a statement on 21 September, the OAS General Secretariat had condemned
this improper use of a referendum because it is illegal according to the 1966 [Agreement to Resolve the Controversy over the Frontier between Venezuela and British Guiana], and because similar misuses of this instrument have served as a pretext in the recent past to try to justify the worst actions between States, including the crime of aggression.
Footnote
520
On 1 December 2023, the Court indicated its provisional measures, among others, asking Venezuela to ‘refrain from taking any action which would modify the situation that currently prevails in the territory in dispute’.
Footnote
521
Notwithstanding the Court’s order, on 3 December 2023, Venezuela went ahead with the referendum and the Venezuelan National Electoral Council claimed based on the preliminary results that the people voted for ‘yes’ more than 95% of the time on each of the five questions on the ballot.
Footnote
522
In a letter dated 6 December to the UN, Guyana claimed that the actions taken by Venezuela in the aftermath of the referendum ‘to formally annex, and incorporate into Venezuelan territory, the Essequibo Region of Guyana’ are ‘flagrant violations of the Court’s Order’ and also
violate the most fundamental principles of international law enshrined in Article 2 (4) of the Charter, which make it unlawful for any State to use or threaten force against the territorial integrity or political independence of another State. The acquisition, or attempted acquisition of another State’s territory constitutes aggression, which violates obligations under peremptory norms of international law. Venezuela is now guilty of breaching all these obligations, and the actions it has announced that it will soon take will only further aggravate the situation. Its conduct plainly constitutes a direct threat to Guyana’s peace and security, and more broadly threatens the peace and security of the entire region.
Footnote
523
Based on the reasons given in its letter, and pursuant to Article 35 of the UN Charter, Guyana also called ‘urgently for a meeting of the Security Council to determine whether the situation described above is likely to endanger the maintenance of international peace and security’.
Footnote
524
In another letter dated 7 December to the UN, Guyana drew attention to ‘a video recording released to the public today by the Minister of Defence of the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela, General Vladimir Padrino Lopez, in which he announces that three Generals have been identified to lead the “conquest of Guayana Esequiba”’.
Footnote
525
Guyana described this as ‘a direct threat of military force against Guyana’s territory and, as such, a grave breach of Article 2 (4) of the Charter of the United Nations, as well as the order of the International Court of Justice of 1 December 2023’.
Footnote
526
In a press conference on 11 December, the Brazilian Defence Minister made clear that Brazil will not allow Venezuelan troops to enter its territory to invade Guyana.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 80 | extracted HTML block 80]
Footnote
527
He stated that ‘[t]hey will only arrive in Guyana by passing through Brazilian territory, and we will not allow that under any circumstances’.
Footnote
528
Tensions between Venezuela and Guyana appeared to have eased after the discussions between the two countries, facilitated by the leaders of the regional countries, resulted in ‘the Joint Declaration of Argyle for Dialogue and Peace between Guyana and Venezuela’ on 14 December 2023, which declared as follows:
1. Agreed that Guyana and Venezuela, directly or indirectly, will not threaten or use force against one another in any circumstances, including those consequential to any existing controversies between the two States.
2. Agreed that any controversies between the two States will be resolved in accordance with international law, including the Geneva Agreement dated February 17, 1966.
(…)
6. Agreed that both States will refrain, whether by words or deeds, from escalating any conflict or disagreement arising from any controversy between them. The two States will cooperate to avoid incidents on the ground conducive to tension between them. In the event of such an incident the two States will immediately communicate with one another, the Caribbean Community (CARICOM), the Community of Latin America and the Caribbean (CELAC), and the President of Brazil to contain, reverse and prevent its recurrence.
Footnote
529
However, towards the end of December, tensions seemed to rise again after Venezuela ordered its armed forces to hold ‘defensive’ military exercises on Venezuela’s eastern Caribbean and Atlantic coasts in response to the UK’s decision to send a warship to Guyana.
Footnote
530
In a television address, the Venezuelan President said that the exercises were being launched ‘in response to the provocation and threat of the United Kingdom against peace and the sovereignty of our country’, adding that the UK’s move was ‘practically a military threat from London’ and broke the ‘spirit’ of the recent agreement reached between Venezuela and Guyana.
Footnote
531
The Guyanese Vice-President responded that the ship’s presence was ‘routine’ and part of building a ‘defensive capability’, adding that ‘We don’t plan on invading Venezuela. President Maduro knows this and he need not have any worry about that’.
Footnote
532
Haiti: The UN Security Council authorises a Multinational Security Support mission
Debates among states on how to respond to Haiti’s request for an international armed force to help quell gang violence in the country continued during the reporting period of this Digest, with experts estimating that, as of August, gangs controlled around 80% of the capital.
Footnote
533

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 81 | extracted HTML block 81]
On 1 August, the OAS General Secretariat welcomed Kenya’s announcement of its willingness to play a leading role in a multinational force to be deployed to Haiti.
Footnote
534
On 4 August, CARICOM welcomed the decision of the Bahamas and Jamaica to join Kenya to contribute to this force.
Footnote
535
CARICOM also hoped ‘that the establishment of the multi-national force will be given full endorsement by the United Nations Security Council as a demonstration of the commitment of the international community to improve the security and humanitarian conditions of the people of Haiti and support restoration of law and order’.
Footnote
536
In a letter dated 14 August to the President of the Security Council, citing the crisis caused by ‘heavily armed gangs capable of mounting robust resistance to anti-gang police operations’, the UN Secretary-General argued that ‘[t]he current context in Haiti is not conducive to peacekeeping’, and accordingly, ‘[n]othing short of the robust use of force, complemented by a suite of non-kinetic measures, by a capable specialized multinational police force enabled by military assets’ would be enough.
Footnote
537
He also reiterated his ‘appeal to potential force-contributing Member States to act now to deploy a non-United Nations multinational force to assist the national police and my appeal to the Security Council to welcome the deployment of this support to Haiti’.
Footnote
538
China and Russia, however, were critical of a potential intervention in Haiti. During the UN Security Council meeting on 6 July, the Chinese representative stated that ‘[w]ithout a legitimate, effective and responsible Government in place, any external efforts to address the crisis are hardly likely to have any lasting effect’.
Footnote
539
Similarly, the Russian representative stated that
We do not entirely understand why, given that political dialogue is stalled in the country, that there is a legitimacy crisis and a lack of control of weapons trafficking, and that the humanitarian plan is underfinanced, some circles consider that the panacea for all Haitian issues would be to send a multinational armed force to the island. The history of foreign interventions in Haiti proves that templates imposed from outside do not take root in Haitian society, that they bring more harm than good and that they are only used as a cover under which to promote the interests of other States. It is therefore not a coincidence that, even now, despite all of the problems, there are voices in Haiti against any kind of foreign interference, and it is our duty to heed these voices and not try to play them down or ignore them.
Footnote
540
In the same meeting, the Special Representative of the Secretary-General for Haiti stated that
‘
[t]he robust international force that Haiti needs must complement and strengthen – not replace – the Haitian National Police, with full respect for Haiti’s national sovereignty’.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 82 | extracted HTML block 82]
Footnote
541
In Resolution adopted on 14 July, the UN Security Council ‘[e]ncourage[d] Member States, including countries in the region, to provide security support to the Haitian National Police in response to the appeal from the Prime Minister of Haiti and the Secretary-General, including through the deployment of a specialized force, upon consultation with Haitian stakeholders’.
Footnote
542
The resolution also ‘[r]equest[ed] the Secretary-General to submit a written report to the Security Council, in consultation with Haiti, within 30 days, outlining the full range of support options the United Nations can provide to enhance the security situation, including (…) support for a non-UN multinational force, or a possible peacekeeping operation, in the context of supporting a political settlement in Haiti’.
Footnote
543
In a letter dated 26 September to the UN, Haiti ‘appreciate[d] the action being taken in the Security Council to ensure that a resolution authorizing a multinational security support mission in Haiti is adopted as soon as possible’ and welcomed
the decision of Kenya to offer to lead the mission and the commitment of other countries to contribute personnel. They have all strongly expressed their desire to obtain Security Council authorization under Chapter VII of the Charter of the United Nations so as to be able to provide support to the mission. For them, as for us, it is either a legal or a political requirement.
Footnote
544
Finally, in Resolution 2699 adopted on 2 October, taking note of Haiti’s request for ‘a specialized international force’ and CARICOM’s appeal to the Council ‘to authorize multinational security efforts’, welcoming Kenya’s decision to lead the mission ‘at the invitation of Haiti’, taking note of Kenya’s call on the Council ‘to approve a resolution under Chapter VII’, and acting under Chapter VII of the UN Charter, the UN Security Council authorised the deployment of
a Multinational Security Support (MSS) mission with a lead country, in close cooperation and coordination with the Government of Haiti (…) on the understanding that the cost of implementing this temporary operation will be borne by voluntary contributions and support from individual Member States and regional organizations.
Footnote
545
The resolution defined the objective of the mission as ‘to support the efforts of the Haitian National Police to re-establish security in Haiti and build security conditions conducive to holding free and fair elections’, among others, by conducting ‘joint security support operations’ with the Haitian National Police, ‘as it works to counter gangs and improve security conditions in Haiti’.
Footnote
546
In the meeting following the adoption of the resolution, the Chinese representative expressed China’s concerns in invoking Chapter VII to authorise the use of force:
Proceeding from the principles of State sovereignty and non-interference in internal affairs, China has always taken a cautious and responsible approach to the Council’s invocation of Chapter VII in authorizing the use of force. There have been some precedents for abuse of that authorization in previous practice. However, in view of the current security situation in Haiti and the concerns and appeals of the international community, especially those of the CARICOM nations, and based on our trust in friendly countries, China has taken a constructive position on the Council’s adoption of resolution 2699 (2023).
Footnote
547
The Russian representative stated that while ‘we have no objections in principle against the initiative as such (…) it must be understood that sending the armed forces of another State to any country, even at that country’s request, is an extreme measure that must be thoroughly thought through’.
Footnote

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 83 | extracted HTML block 83]
548
The representative also complained about the lack of detailed information on ‘the modalities for the use of force’ and expressed Russia’s concern against ‘a blind invocation of Chapter VII’.
Footnote
549
In the days following the authorisation of the UN Security Council, however, Kenya’s courts first temporarily suspended,
Footnote
550
and later extended the suspension of,
Footnote
551
the Kenyan government’s plans to deploy around 1000 police officers to Haiti after the government’s decision was challenged on constitutional grounds. Moreover, on 9 November, the Kenyan Interior Minister announced on the social media platform
X
that ‘Kenya will not allow its troops to exit the Country for Haiti until all the required resources, including equipment and finances, are mobilised and availed’, adding that ‘[t]he cost of deployment will be borne by United Nations member states through voluntary contributions’.
Footnote
552
Guatemala – Belize: Tensions over territorial dispute
In the meeting of the UN General Assembly on 23 September, the representative of Belize stated that
As Belize and Guatemala continue to pursue the peaceful, just and final resolution of Guatemala’s claim to Belizean territory before the International Court of Justice, illegal incursions by Guatemalans continue. Recently, Guatemalan military forces illegally entered Belizean waters and unlawfully removed Belizean flags placed on Belizean soil. Deforestation, cattle ranching and the cultivation of illegal drugs by Guatemalans continue in remote and protected areas of Belize. Those activities are contrary to international law and goodneighbourliness.
Footnote
553
In response, the Guatemalan representative emphasised ‘the joint work of the armed forces of both countries’, and Guatemala’s role ‘as a leading country’, in the fight against drug trafficking, and accused the ‘Belizean radical pro-independence civil society groups’ of provoking ‘pockets of instability in the Sarstoon River area, thereby violating the territorial integrity and sovereignty of our country’.
Footnote
554
The representative also complained that
Throughout our history, we have witnessed the deaths of Guatemalan nationals who perished at the hands of the Belizean armed forces. However, despite the constant provocations by such groups, there has never been a documented case of Guatemalan soldiers violating the human rights of fellow Belizeans or disturbing the peace.

[SRC-1ca70efabd2771c7 UNIT 84 | extracted HTML block 84]
Footnote
555
Accusations and counteraccusations between the two countries continued during the UN General Assembly meeting on 29 September.
Footnote
556
6. Non-Regional Issues
UN Security Council briefing on the theme ‘Artificial intelligence: opportunities and risks for international peace and security’
On 18 July 2023, the United Kingdom organized a first-ever briefing in the UN Security Council on the theme ‘Artificial intelligence: opportunities and risks for international peace and security’.
Footnote
557
Some of the remarks made by States during the debate are relevant also for the
jus ad bellum
framework. In this context, it is worth noting the words of the representative of Brazil who claimed that ‘[t]he military applications of AI, especially with regard to the use of force, should strictly abide by international humanitarian law, as enshrined in the Geneva Conventions and other pertinent international commitments’.
Footnote
558
On the other hand, the representative of Russia said that in preparing for the meeting he asked artificial intelligence (AI) itself if AI is a threat to international peace and security. He received the response that ‘[a]rtificial intelligence can pose certain threats to international peace and security, but it can also have a positive impact’. One of the issues indicated by AI was autonomous weapon systems as ‘[t]he development of AI-based autonomous weapon systems could pose security risks, as such systems could decide to use force without direct human involvement. That could lead to unpredictable consequences and exacerbate armed conflicts’.
Footnote
559

No potential conflict of interest was reported by the author(s).
